However, processing 1M token files requires a hierarchical chunking strategy since even the best long-context models max out around 128K-200K tokens practically. The optimal setup combines **Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct-4bit** for maximum parallelism or **Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-4bit** for better quality, both with native 128K context windows.\n\n---\n\n## Top model recommendations ranked by your priorities\n\nFor your use case prioritizing speed and parallelism over capability, here are the specific models to use:\n\n**Fastest option (maximum parallel instances):**\n| Model | Size | Context | Memory | Speed | Instances in 64GB |\n|-------|------|---------|--------|-------|-------------------|\n| `mlx-community/Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct-4bit` | 0.5B | 128K | ~0.3GB | 400+ tok/s | 15-20 |\n| `mlx-community/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct-4bit` | 1.5B | 128K | ~0.9GB | 200-300 tok/s | 12-15 |\n| `mlx-community/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct-4bit` | 1B | 128K | ~0.6GB | 300-600 tok/s | 15-20 |\n\n**Best balance (quality + speed + parallelism):**\n| Model | Size | Context | Memory | Speed | Instances in 64GB |\n|-------|------|---------|--------|-------|-------------------|\n| `mlx-community/Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct-4bit` | 3B | **128K** | ~1.7GB | 100-150 tok/s | **8-10** |\n| `mlx-community/Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct-4bit` | 3B | **128K** | ~1.8GB | 100-150 tok/s | **8-10** |\n| `mlx-community/Phi-3.5-mini-instruct-4bit` | 3.8B | **128K** | ~2.2GB | 80-120 tok/s | **6-8** |\n\n**Higher quality (fewer parallel instances):**\n| Model | Size | Context | Memory | Speed | Instances in 64GB |\n|-------|------|---------|--------|-------|-------------------|\n| `mlx-community/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-4bit` | 7B | **128K** | ~4.0GB | 85-95 tok/s | **4-5** |\n| `mlx-community/Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct-4bit` | 8B | **128K** | ~4.7GB | 85-95 tok/s | **4** |\n\nThe **Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct-4bit** emerges as the optimal choice\u2014it offers native 128K context, excellent MLX optimization, fits 8-10 parallel instances in 64GB, and delivers 100-150 tok/s per instance.\n\n---\n\n## Critical reality check on 1 million token files\n\nNo current model can process 1M tokens in a single pass on your hardware. Here's why and what to do:\n\n**Maximum practical context lengths on 64GB:**\n- **7B model at 128K context**: Requires ~25-35GB (model + KV cache)\n- **3B model at 128K context**: Requires ~15-20GB\n- **Memory formula**: KV cache scales linearly with context; a 7B model needs ~2GB per 4K context increment\n\n**The math problem**: Your 1M token files would require approximately **250GB+ of KV cache alone** at full context, far exceeding 64GB. Even Qwen2.5-1M models (which claim 1M context via sparse attention) require specialized vLLM optimizations not available in MLX.\n\n**The solution**: Hierarchical chunking with map-reduce processing. This is not a limitation\u2014it's actually the standard production approach used even by systems with unlimited memory because attention scales quadratically (O(n\u00b2)) with context length.\n\n---\n\n## Optimal processing architecture for your 600 files\n\nFor batch processing 600 files of 1M tokens each, implement this three-stage pipeline:\n\n**Stage 1: Chunk each document**\n```python\n# Split 1M tokens into 500-token chunks with 100-token overlap\n# Result: ~2,000 chunks per document\nfrom langchain.text_splitter import TokenTextSplitter\nsplitter = TokenTextSplitter(chunk_size=500, chunk_overlap=100)\n```\n\n**Stage 2: Parallel map phase**\n- Run **8 parallel instances** of `Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct-4bit`\n- Process chunks in batches of 50-100 per instance\n- Generate 50-100 token summaries per chunk\n- At 100 tok/s per instance \u00d7 8 instances = **800 tok/s aggregate throughput**\n\n**Stage 3: Hierarchical reduce phase**\n- Group every 20 chunk summaries \u2192 generate section summary\n- Combine section summaries \u2192 document summary  \n- 3 levels of recursive summarization handles arbitrary length\n\n**Estimated processing time:**\n- Per 1M token document: ~30-60 minutes (including all summarization levels)\n- 600 documents with 8 parallel instances: **50-75 hours total**\n\n---\n\n## Memory budget for parallel instances\n\nBased on benchmark data, here's exactly how to allocate your 64GB:\n\n| Configuration | Model Memory | KV Cache (8K context) | System | Available Instances |\n|--------------|--------------|----------------------|--------|---------------------|\n| Qwen2.5-3B-4bit \u00d7 8 | 13.6GB | 16GB | 10GB | \u2705 **8 instances** |\n| Qwen2.5-7B-4bit \u00d7 4 | 16GB | 16GB | 10GB | \u2705 **4 instances** |\n| Llama-3.2-1B-4bit \u00d7 15 | 9GB | 30GB | 10GB | \u2705 **15 instances** |\n\n**Key insight**: Use shorter working context (4K-8K) for chunk processing rather than full 128K\u2014this dramatically reduces per-instance memory while still leveraging the model's training on long sequences.\n\n---\n\n## Performance benchmarks on Apple Silicon\n\nReal-world MLX benchmarks from M2/M3 Ultra Mac Studios:\n\n| Model | Quantization | Token Generation | Prompt Processing |\n|-------|--------------|------------------|-------------------|\n| Gemma 3 1B | Q4 | **237 tok/s** | 800+ tok/s |\n| Gemma 3 4B | Q4 | **134 tok/s** | 600+ tok/s |\n| Qwen3-0.6B | Q8 | **402 tok/s** | 1000+ tok/s |\n| Llama-3.2-1B | Q4 | **300-600 tok/s** | 1000+ tok/s |\n| Llama 7B | Q4 | **88-94 tok/s** | 1200+ tok/s |\n| Llama 7B | Q8 | **60-67 tok/s** | 1000+ tok/s |\n\n**Quantization impact**: Q4 provides **2.3x faster generation** than FP16 due to reduced memory bandwidth requirements. On Mac Studio's 800 GB/s unified memory, this is the dominant factor for inference speed.\n\n**MLX vs alternatives**: MLX achieves ~230 tok/s sustained throughput on M3 Ultra, outperforming llama.cpp (~150 tok/s) and Ollama (~20-40 tok/s) in optimized scenarios.\n\n---\n\n## MLX-specific optimizations to implement\n\n**Prompt caching** saves compute when processing multiple queries against the same context:\n```bash\n# Cache the shared context once\ncat document_context.txt | mlx_lm.cache_prompt \\\n    --model mlx-community/Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct-4bit \\\n    --prompt-cache-file cached_context.safetensors\n\n# Reuse for multiple queries\nmlx_lm.generate --prompt-cache-file cached_context.safetensors \\\n    --prompt \"Summarize the key findings.\"\n```\n\n**Rotating KV cache** enables longer sequences with fixed memory:\n```bash\nmlx_lm.generate --model mlx-community/Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct-4bit \\\n    --max-kv-size 4096  # Fixed cache size\n```\n\n**Batch generation** (available in mlx-lm) provides throughput improvements with sublinear scaling\u201432x batch size costs only ~9x more time on M2 Max.\n\n---\n\n## Recommended implementation code\n\n```python\nfrom mlx_lm import load, generate\nfrom multiprocessing import Pool\nimport os\n\n# Load model once per worker\ndef init_worker():\n    global model, tokenizer\n    model, tokenizer = load(\"mlx-community/Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct-4bit\")\n\ndef process_chunk(chunk_text):\n    prompt = f\"Summarize this text in 2-3 sentences:\\n\\n{chunk_text}\"\n    return generate(model, tokenizer, prompt=prompt, max_tokens=100)\n\n# Process with parallel workers\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    chunks = load_all_chunks()  # Your 500-token chunks\n    \n    with Pool(processes=8, initializer=init_worker) as pool:\n        summaries = pool.map(process_chunk, chunks)\n```\n\n---\n\n## Conclusion\n\nFor your Mac Studio with 64GB processing 600 files of 1M tokens, use **Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct-4bit** running **8 parallel instances** with hierarchical map-reduce chunking. This configuration delivers approximately **800 aggregate tokens/second**, processes your entire corpus in roughly 50-75 hours, and leaves headroom for system operations. The key insight is that chunking isn't a workaround\u2014it's the correct architectural pattern for documents exceeding 128K tokens, even on systems with unlimited memory, because it transforms O(n\u00b2) attention complexity into O(n) processing. Start with 500-token chunks, generate per-chunk summaries in parallel, then recursively merge summaries through 2-3 hierarchy levels to produce coherent document-level outputs.",

        "text": "The \u201c1M\u201d suffix indicates the ability of these models to handle input sequences of up to one million tokens. There is an API-accessible model, Qwen2.5-Turbo, which is based on a Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture.\n\nThe Qwen2.5-1M series are the first open-source Qwen models capable of processing up to 1 million tokens. This leap in context length allows these models to tackle more complex, real-world scenarios that require extensive information processing or generation. Traditional LLMs have been limited by the amount of text they can process at one time. This limitation restricts their ability to handle complex tasks requiring extensive information or long-range dependencies. The limited context window is a drawback in tasks such as: Code generation and debugging that relies on repository-level context \u00b7 In-depth research based on large volumes of documents \u00b7 Comprehending and generating long-range dependencies within text \u00b7 Models like the GPT series, Llama series, and the earlier Qwen models have rapidly expanded their context windows from 4k or 8k tokens to 128k tokens.\n\nThe Qwen2.5-1M series includes two open-source instruction-tuned models: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-1M and Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct-1M. The \u201c1M\u201d suffix indicates the ability of these models to handle input sequences of up to one million tokens. There is an API-accessible model, Qwen2.5-Turbo, which is based on a Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture. Qwen2.5-Turbo delivers performance comparable to GPT-4o-mini, while offering longer context and more competitive pricing. The Qwen2.5-1M series incorporates several key features: Enhanced Long-Context Capabilities: Significant improvements in handling long-range dependencies and complex information. Efficient Inference Framework: An open-sourced inference framework optimized for long-context processing, which allows for cost-effective deployment. Length Extrapolation Method: Allows models trained on 256k context lengths to seamlessly scale up to 1M contexts without additional training.\n\nThe Qwen2.5-1M series of models were evaluated on the Passkey Retrieval task with a 1 million token context, with models successfully retrieving hidden numbers in the extensive context. For more complex long-context tasks, the models were evaluated using three benchmarks: RULER: Measures the ability to find multiple \u201cneedles\u201d or answer multiple questions within irrelevant contexts. LV-Eval: Tests the model\u2019s ability to understand numerous evidence fragments simultaneously. Longbench-Chat: Evaluates human preference alignment in long-context tasks. The Qwen2.5-1M series models significantly outperformed their 128k counterparts in most long-context tasks, particularly for sequences exceeding 64k in length. The Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct-1M model consistently outperformed GPT-4o-mini across multiple datasets. Qwen2.5-Turbo achieved a good balance of performance, speed, and cost-effectiveness.\n\nThe Time to First Token (TTFT) for different context lengths was measured on Nvidia H20 and A100 GPUs, showing that the sparse attention and optimized inference engine achieved a 3.2 to 6.7 times speedup when processing a 1M token context. The Qwen2.5-Turbo model achieved shorter processing times and lower costs compared to the open-source models while still achieving competitive results on both short and long context tasks. ... GPUs with Ampere or Hopper architecture that support optimized kernels. CUDA Version 12.1 or 12.3. Python Version >= 3.9 and <= 3.12. VRAM requirements for processing 1 million token sequences: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-1M: At least 120GB VRAM (total across GPUs). Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct-1M: At least 320GB VRAM (total across GPUs). If the available VRAM is not sufficient, the models can still be used for shorter tasks. The vLLM repository needs to be cloned from a custom branch and installed manually.",

This breakthrough allows for deep document retrieval, long-term conversational memory, and enhanced multi-step reasoning. In this article, we explore how Qwen2.5-1M is reshaping AI capabilities. Despite advancements in AI, existing Large Language Models (LLMs) like GPT-4o, Claude 3, and Llama-3 are still constrained by a 128K token limit. This creates challenges such as:: Processing lengthy documents or books without truncation. Maintaining conversational memory throughout extensive interactions. Performing deep document retrieval and multi-step reasoning effectively. This is where Alibaba\u2019s Qwen2.5-1M changes the game. Introducing an unprecedented 1 million token context window overcomes these limitations, making it the first open-source LLM to achieve this scale.\n\nContextual Forgetting: LLMs trained on shorter sequences struggle to retain long-range dependencies, causing information loss over extended prompts. How Qwen2.5-1M\u2019s 1 Million Token Context Window Changes AI? So, how does a 1 million token context window make a difference in the real world? Let\u2019s explore key industries where this advancement transforms AI applications. Figure 1: Passkey Retrieval Performance of Qwen2.5-1M Models on Documents up to 1 Million Tokens This test assesses the model\u2019s capability to extract a concealed number from ultra-long documents filled with extraneous content. The results indicate that Qwen2.5-1M models can accurately retrieve hidden numbers from documents containing up to 1 million tokens, with minimal errors observed in the 7B model. To understand how Qwen2.5-1M achieves this breakthrough, let\u2019s explore its underlying architecture and optimizations.\n\nQwen2.5-1M enhances the Qwen2.5 series by utilizing an optimized Transformer-based architecture, which effectively scales to 1 million tokens while maintaining both inference speed and memory efficiency. Key Architectural Characteristics. Grouped Query Attention (GQA): Reduces KV cache memory usage while improving attention computation efficiency. SwiGLU Activation: Enhances non-linearity and optimizes model convergence. Rotary Positional Embeddings (RoPE): Enables smooth scaling to long contexts while preserving positional encoding efficiency. QKV Bias and RMSNorm: Enhances training stability and inference precision. ... These optimizations make Qwen2.5-1M significantly more efficient at handling long-range dependencies than previous models. Building an AI model that understands 1 million tokens at once demands extensive training on vast datasets.\n\nTraining an LLM directly on 1M-token sequences would be computationally prohibitive. Instead, Qwen2.5-1M follows a five-stage expansion strategy, gradually increasing context length: Adaptive Base Frequency (ABF) dynamically adjusts RoPE encodings, enabling smooth extrapolation to longer token sequences. 75% max-length sequences and 25% shorter sequences ensure cross-context generalization. It prevents memory bottlenecks early in training, ensures computational feasibility by incrementally scaling context length, and maintains generalization across different sequence lengths. Post-training is crucial for enhancing Qwen2.5-1 M\u2019s performance, ensuring it makes the most of its 1 million-token context length while maintaining its ability to handle shortively. Advanced techniques such as synthetic instruction tuning, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning (RL) are employed to optimize the model for real-world applications, targeting tasks like multi-hop reasoning, document summa",

Here, we\u2019ll briefly introduce the key techniques behind building Qwen2.5-1M. For more details, please check out our technical report. Training with long sequences demands substantial computational resources, so we adopt a progressive approach to expand the context length for Qwen2.5-1M through multiple stages: We \n\nStage 1: Fine-tuned only on short instructions (up to 32K tokens) using the same data and steps as the 128K versions of Qwen2.5. Stage 2: Mixed short (up to 32K) and long (up to 256K) instructions to enhance long-context task performance while maintaining short-task quality. In Reinforcement Learning, we train models on short texts up to 8K tokens, which sufficiently improves alignment with human preferences and generalizes well to long-context tasks. The final instruction-tuned models are capable of handling sequences up to 256K tokens. During training, we develop an instruction-tuned model with a context length of 256K tokens.           "type": "webpage_metadata",

        "text": "We introduce Qwen2.5-1M, a series of models that extend the context length to 1 million tokens. Compared to the previous 128K version, the Qwen2.5-1M series have significantly enhanced long-context capabilities through long-context pre-training ...\n\nAbstract:We introduce Qwen2.5-1M, a series of models that extend the context length to 1 million tokens. Compared to the previous 128K version, the Qwen2.5-1M series have significantly enhanced long-context capabilities through long-context pre-training and post-training. Key techniques such as long data synthesis, progressive pre-training, and multi-stage supervised fine-tuning are employed to effectively enhance long-context performance while reducing training costs. To promote the use of long-context models among a broader user base, we present and open-source our inference framework. This framework includes a length extrapolation method that can expand the model context lengths by at least four times, or even more, without additional training. To reduce inference costs, we implement a sparse attention method along with chunked prefill optimization for deployment scenarios and a sparsity refinement method to improve precision.\n\nAbstract page for arXiv paper 2501.15383: Qwen2.5-1M Technical Report\n\nAdditionally, we detail our optimizations in the inference engine, including kernel optimization, pipeline parallelism, and scheduling optimization, which significantly enhance overall inference performance. By leveraging our inference framework, the Qwen2.5-1M models achieve a remarkable 3x to 7x prefill speedup in scenarios with 1 million tokens of context. This framework provides an efficient and powerful solution for developing applications that require long-context processing using open-source models. The Qwen2.5-1M series currently includes the open-source models Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-1M and Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct-1M, as well as the API-accessed model Qwen2.5-Turbo. Evaluations show that Qwen2.5-1M models have been greatly improved in long-context tasks without compromising performance in short-context scenarios. Specifically, the Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct-1M model significantly outperforms GPT-4o-mini in long-context tasks and supports contexts eight times longer.",

For instance, Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B/14B/32B-Base performs as well as Qwen2.5-3B/7B/14B/32B/72B-Base, respectively. Notably, in areas like STEM, coding, and reasoning, Qwen3 dense base models even outperform larger Qwen2.5 models. For Qwen3-MoE base models, they achieve similar performance to Qwen2.5 dense base models while using only 10% of the active parameters. This results in significant savings in both training and inference costs. To develop the hybrid model capable of both step-by-step reasoning and rapid responses, we implemented a four-stage training pipeline. This pipeline includes: (1) long chain-of-thought (CoT) cold start, (2) reasoning-based reinforcement learning (RL), (3) thinking mode fusion, and (4) general RL.",

        "text": "From the diagram, we can infer that Qwen 3 0.6B,1.7B,4B were trained through a Strong-to-Weak Distillation process, which is part of the pipeline for creating Lightweight Models. Here\u2019s a step-by-step breakdown of the training process: Base Models: The process begins with pre-trained Base Models, which act as the foundation for subsequent training and distillation. ... Base Models are first trained through a multi-stage process to create Frontier Models like Qwen3\u2013235B-A22B and Qwen3\u201332B. ... Stage 1 (Long-CoT Cold Start): Initial training with long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. Stage 2 (Reasoning RL): Reinforcement learning (RL) to enhance reasoning capabilities. Stage 3 (Thinking Mode Fusion): Integration of Thinking Modes (e.g., reasoning and quick-response modes). Stage 4 (General RL): General reinforcement learning for broader capabilities.\n\nQwen 3 0.6B: Why Small Models Still Matter Today Qwen 3 0.6B is designed for one core mission \u2014 solving specific, high-frequency tasks at minimal cost and latency. While massive models dominate \u2026\n\nFrom the diagram, we can infer that Qwen 3 0.6B,1.7B,4B were trained through a Strong-to-Weak Distillation process, which is part of the pipeline for creating Lightweight Models. Here\u2019s a step-by-step breakdown of the training process: Base Models: The process begins with pre-trained Base Models, which act as the foundation for subsequent training and distillation. ... Base Models are first trained through a multi-stage process to create Frontier Models like Qwen3\u2013235B-A22B and Qwen3\u201332B. ... Stage 1 (Long-CoT Cold Start): Initial training with long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. Stage 2 (Reasoning RL): Reinforcement learning (RL) to enhance reasoning capabilities. Stage 3 (Thinking Mode Fusion): Integration of Thinking Modes (e.g., reasoning and quick-response modes). Stage 4 (General RL): General reinforcement learning for broader capabilities.\n\nThe large Frontier Models (e.g., Qwen3\u2013235B and Qwen3\u201332B) are then used as teacher models to guide the training of Lightweight Models like Qwen3\u20134B. This distillation process ensures that the smaller models retain the knowledge and performance of the larger models while significantly reducing size and computational requirements. ... As a result of this distillation process, Qwen 3 0.6B,1.7B,4B are a lightweight version, benefiting from the knowledge of the larger models while being optimized for efficiency. ... Overall, Qwen 3 0.6b performs well in reasoning \u2014 and quantitative \u2014 reasoning \u2014 related tests, but shows subpar performance in coding \u2014 related tests. Use Cases: Search ranking, ad delivery, real-time chat \u2014 scenarios requiring millions of queries per day.         "text": "A KV-cache representing a 128k token context window for a single user (batch size 1) consumes about 40 GB of memory with Llama 3 70B, and this scales linearly with the number of users.\n\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) are at the forefront of AI innovation, but their massive size can complicate inference efficiency. Models such as Llama 3 70B and\u2026\n\nDuring inference, these models typically require additional data structures such as the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with context length and batch size. A KV-cache representing a 128k token context window for a single user (batch size 1) consumes about 40 GB of memory with Llama 3 70B, and this scales linearly with the number of users. In a production deployment, attempting to load such large models entirely into GPU memory could result in an out-of-memory (OOM) error. The CPU and GPU in NVIDIA Grace Blackwell and NVIDIA Grace Hopper architectures are connected with an NVIDIA NVLink C2C, a 900 GB/s, memory-coherent interconnect that delivers 7x the bandwidth of PCIe Gen 5. NVLink-C2C memory coherency creates a single unified memory address space shared by both the CPU and the GPU (Figure 1), enabling them to access and operate on the same data without explicit data transfers or redundant memory copies.\n\nTo begin, we need to set up our environment and gain access to the Llama 3 70B model. Note that the following code samples are designed to run on an NVIDIA Grace Hopper GH200 Superchip machine to show the benefits of its unified memory architecture. These same techniques also work on NVIDIA Grace Blackwell-based systems. ... Request model access from Hugging Face: Visit the Llama 3 70B model page on Hugging Face to request access. Open Jupyter notebook on the GH200 machine and run the following commands: #Install huggingface and cuda packages !pip install --upgrade huggingface_hub !pip install transformers !pip install nvidia-cuda-runtime-cu12 \u00b7\n\nThe NVIDIA Grace Blackwell and NVIDIA Grace Hopper architectures use NVLink-C2C, a 900 GB/s memory-coherent interconnect, to create a unified memory address space shared by the CPU and GPU, allowing them to access and operate on the same data without explicit data transfers. Large Language Models (LLMs) like Llama 3 70B require significant memory, and loading them into GPU memory can result in out-of-memory (OOM) errors, but unified memory architecture enables the CPU and GPU to share memory, resolving this issue. By using managed memory allocations with the RAPIDS Memory Manager (RMM) library, developers can allocate memory accessible from both the CPU and GPU, enabling workloads to exceed the physical GPU memory limit without manual data transfers on systems like the NVIDIA GH200 Grace Hopper Superchip.         "title": "Techniques for KV Cache Optimization in Large Language Models",
        "url": "https://www.omrimallis.com/posts/techniques-for-kv-cache-optimization/",
        "metadata": {
          "type": "webpage_metadata",
          "site_domain": "omrimallis.com",

        "text": "If you are familiar with Numbers every LLM Developer should know, they claim each output token requires approximately 1MB of GPU memory. That\u2019s where this number comes from. Now, this calculation is for each token.\n\nIf you are familiar with Numbers every LLM Developer should know, they claim each output token requires approximately 1MB of GPU memory. That\u2019s where this number comes from. Now, this calculation is for each token. To accommodate the full context size for a single inference task, we must allocate enough cache space accordingly. Moreover, if we run inference in batches (i.e. on multiple prompts simultaneously once), the cache size is multiplied again. Therefore, the full size of the cache is: 2 * 2 * head_dim * n_heads * n_layers * max_context_length * batch_size \u00b7 If we want to utilize the entire Llama-2-13B context of 4096 tokens, in batches of 8, the size of the cache would be 25GB, almost as much as the 26GB needed to store the model parameters. That\u2019s tons of GPU memory! ... The maximum context size that can be supported. The maximum size of each inference batch.\n\nFor example, assuming GPT-4\u2019s per-token memory is 1MB (a pure guess), utilizing the full context would necessitate approximately 128GB of GPU memory, exceeding the capacity of a single A100 card. Distributed inference involves running LLM requests across multiple GPUs. While this offers additional advantages, it also enables the KV cache to exceed the memory of a single GPU. The way it works is, at least theoretically, rather simple: Since the self-attention mechanism is composed of multiple heads working independently, it can be distributed across multiple GPUs. Each GPU is assigned a subset of attention heads to execute. The key and value vectors for each attention head are then cached in the memory of the allocated GPU. When finished, the results of all attention heads are collected to a single GPU where they are combined for use in the rest of the transformer layer.\n\nThe remarkable capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) come with significant computational challenges, especially concerning GPU memory usage. One source of these challenges lies in what\u2019s referred to as the Key-Value (KV) cache, a critical optimization technique employed in LLMs to ensure efficient token-by-token generation. This cache consumes significant amount of GPU memory to the point that it itself can limit LLM performance and context size. This post covers KV cache optimization techniques. It starts with an explanation of how a basic KV cache works and then delves into various methods implemented by open-source models and frameworks to enhance its scalability and reduce its memory footprint. I will assume a basic understanding of transformers and self-attention, though the most crucial concepts will be reviewed. The Illustrated Transformer is also a good source for beginners.\n\nDuring inference, an LLM generates its output token-by-token, a process known as autoregressive decoding. Each token generated depends on all previous tokens, including the tokens in the prompt and all previously generated output tokens. When this list of tokens grows large due to a lengthy prompt or a long output, the computations in the self-attention stage can become a bottleneck. The KV cache resolves this bottleneck, maintaining a small and consistent performance for each decoding step, regardless of the token count. To see why it\u2019s needed, recall that in a standard self-attention mechanism as originally proposed and used in models such as Llama-7B, three vectors are computed for each token, known as key, query and value vectors. These vectors are calculated using a simple matrix multiplication between the token\u2019s embedding and the WK, WQ and WV matrices, which are part of the model\u2019s learned parameters.",

The KV cache, in particular, can expand substantially with longer text contexts and more complex model structures. This becomes a critical challenge for applications that handle extensive contexts, like retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems. In this article, I\u2019ll discuss the KV cache and the process of quantizing it for Llama 3. In transformer models, text is generated one token at a time, with each new prediction building upon the context of all previously gene\n\nThe KV cache for one token occupies 131,072 bytes, i.e., 0.1 MB. It seems small but for many different types of applications, LLMs are required to generate thousands of tokens. For instance, if we want to exploit the full context size of Llama 3 8B, which is 8192, the KV cache will store the KV tensors for 8191 tokens. That\u2019s 1.1 GB. For a consumer GPU with 24 GB of RAM, it\u2019s 4.5% of its total memory that will be occupied by the KV cache. For larger models, the KV cache grows faster. For instance, for Llama 3 70B which has 80 layers, our equation becomes: ... For 8191 tokens, the KV cache of Llama 3 70B would occupy 2.7 GB. Note also that this is the memory consumption for one sequence. If we do batch decoding, we have to multiply this value by the batch size. For instance, using a batch size of 32 with Llama 3 8B requires 35.2 GB of GPU RAM. This can\u2019t be done with one consumer GPU.\n\nresidual_length: This parameter specifies how many tokens at the beginning of the sequence should not be quantized to maintain model accuracy. A setting of 64 strikes a good balance between accuracy and memory usage. device: It\u2019s advisable to explicitly set this to the same device where the model is loaded. Allowing the Transformers library to auto-manage this might lead to inefficiencies, such as defaulting to CPU usage even when the model is GPU-loaded. By configuring these hyperparameters correctly, the quality of the generated outputs should remain consistent, whether or not the KV cache is quantized. Regarding memory savings, significant differences become apparent particularly when processing large batches and extensive sequences. In conclusion, quantizing the KV cache offers a significant method to decrease the memory requirements of LLMs during inference.",

KV caching, a deeper look In the previous post, we introduced KV caching, a common optimization of the inference process of LLMs that make compute requirements of the \u2026\n\nIn any case, all the cases above assume a single host, we are still bounded by the storage capacity of the largest multi-GPU instance we can put our hands on. To the best of my knowledge, no inference framework support multi-host model parallelism yet. If we were able to shard both the model and the KV cache on multiple hosts, the amount of available memory and the maximum sequence length we would be capable to process become virtually unlimited. This is the problem that the Infinite-LLM paper [26] aims to solve by both introducing a new distributed attention algorithm (DistAttention) and adapting the Ray framework to build a multi-host distributed KV cache management and scheduling system (DistKV-LLM). In this post, we learned how opting for KV caching creates additional challenges. The KV cache of multi-head attention (MHA) models indeed consumes a lot of GPU memory, in the order of ~1MB/token, and can easily grow larger than the model weights.\n\nIn the previous post, we introduced KV caching, a common optimization of the inference process of LLMs that make compute requirements of the (self-)attention mechanism to scale linearly rather than quadratically in the total sequence length (prompt + generated completions). More concretely, KV caching consists to spare the recomputation of key and value tensors of past tokens at each generation step by storing (\u201dcaching\u201d) these tensors in GPU memory as they get computed along the generation process. KV caching is a compromise: we trade memory against compute. In this post, we will see how big the KV cache can grow, what challenges it creates and what are the most common strategies used to tackle them. This is quite simple: for each token of each sequence in the batch, we need to store two vector tensors (one key tensor and one value tensor) of size d_head for each attention head of each attention layer.\n\nLet\u2019s focus on Llama-2\u20137B. Using half precision, loading the model weights consumes ~14GB of memory, same as a caching keys and values for 28k tokens. 28k tokens could for example correspond to a batch of 56 sequences of length 512 which is not particularly extreme. We can see from the numbers above that the KV cache memory consumption can grow very large and even exceed the amount of memory required to load the model weights for large sequences. This calculator provides a theoretical estimation to give you a ballpark idea of VRAM requirements. It does not provide exact accuracy down to the gigabyte due to many variables (e.g., memory bandwidth, CUDA kernel implementations, driver overheads, minor model architecture differences) that are too complex to add into this calculator. However, it is generally accurate enough for hardware planning and capacity estimation.\n\nIt's a common misconception that Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models use less VRAM during inference. While only a subset of \"experts\" are active for any given token, all experts must reside in memory (VRAM) to enable fast switching and maintain performance. The main resource benefit of MoE is in reducing compute requirements (especially during training), not memory usage. However, MoE models do provide better generation speed compared to dense models with the same memory footprint, since they activate fewer parameters per token. If you observe an MoE model using less VRAM, it is likely due to other factors, such as quantization, rather than the MoE architecture itself. ... This underscores the remarkable ability of DCA to extend supported context lengths, without any training required. For long-context language models, inference speed is crucial for user experience. We introduce a sparse attention mechanism based on MInference to accelerate the prefill phase.\n\nIntegrating with Chunked Prefill: Directly processing sequences of 1M tokens results in substantial memory overhead to store the activations in MLP layers, consuming 71GB of VRAM in Qwen2.5-7B. By integrating with chunk prefill with a chunk length of 32,768 tokens, activation VRAM usage is reduced by 96.7%, leading to a significant decrease in memory consumption. Integrating with Length Extrapolation: We integrate DCA with MInference in long-context processing, thereby enhancing inference efficiency and achieving greater accuracy. Sparsity Refinement on Long Sequences: MInference requires an offline search to determine the optimal sparsification configuration for each attention head. Due to the computational demand of full attention weights, this search is typically conducted on short sequences, which may not generalize well to longer sequences. We developed a method to refine the sparsification configuration specifically for sequences up to 1M tokens, which significantly reduces the a\n\nIn Pretraining, we gradually increase the context length from 4K to 256K tokens while using Adjusted Base Frequency, raising the RoPE base from 10,000 to 10,000,000. In Supervised Fine-tuning, we split this into two stages to preserve performance on shorter sequences: Stage 1: Fine-tuned only on short instructions (up to 32K tokens) using the same data and steps as the 128K versions of Qwen2.5. Stage 2: Mixed short (up to 32K) and long (up to 256K) instructions to enhance long-context task performance while maintaining short-task quality. In Reinforcement Learning, we train models on short texts up to 8K tokens, which sufficiently improves alignment with human preferences and generalizes well to long-context tasks. The final instruction-tuned models are capable of handling sequences up to 256K tokens. During training, we develop an instruction-tuned model with a context length of 256K tokens.         "type": "knowledge",
        "title": "How LLMs Scaled from 512 to 2M Context: A Technical Deep Dive",
        "url": "https://amaarora.github.io/posts/2025-09-21-rope-context-extension.html",
        "metadata": {
          "type": "webpage_metadata",
          "site_domain": "github.io",
          "favicon_url": "https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=github.io",
          "site_name": "Amaarora"

        "is_missing": false,
        "text": "The beauty of dynamic scaling is that it eliminates the need to choose between short and long sequence performance - the model automatically adapts to whatever it needs to process. In fact, this approach has been adopted in production: Qwen models use Dynamic NTK-aware scaling to achieve strong performance across context lengths (Qwen Team 2023). Qwen Team. 2023. \u201cQwen Technical Memo: Long Context Inference.\u201d https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen/blob/main/tech_memo.md#long-context-inference. After the initial NTK-aware scaling, bloc97 discovered that different frequency components benefit from different scaling strategies. This led to the \u201cby parts\u201d correction - a more sophisticated approach that applies different interpolation methods to different parts of the frequency spectrum. The key insight: RoPE\u2019s frequency components encode information at different scales: High frequencies (low dimensions): Encode local, fine-grained relationships \u00b7\n\nFigure 4: Perplexity comparison of different context extension methods on LLaMA 7B. Gray line: baseline (scale=1), Blue dashed: linear interpolation (scale=4), Green solid: NTK-aware scaling (\u03b1=8). NTK-aware scaling maintains much lower perplexity across extended context lengths without any fine-tuning. The elegance of NTK-aware scaling lies in recognizing that RoPE\u2019s rotation frequencies, not positions themselves, are the right abstraction level for context extension. Shortly after NTK-aware scaling, Reddit user emozilla proposed an elegant solution to the fixed scaling tradeoff: adjust the scaling factor dynamically based on the actual sequence length (emozilla 2023). emozilla. 2023. \u201cDynamically Scaled RoPE Further Increases Performance of Long Context LLaMA with Zero Fine-Tuning.\u201d https://www.reddit.com/r/LocalLLaMA/comments/14mrgpr/dynamically_scaled_rope_further_increases/. Both Position Interpolation and NTK-aware scaling require choosing a fixed scaling factor upfront: Choose a\n\nWe follow this up with rotary embeddings (RoPE), and also get an intuitive understanding of simply thinking in complex number terms, helps embed position information into embedding vectors through rotation. RoPE encodes the absolute position with a rotation matrix and meanwhile incorporates the explicit relative position dependency in self-attention formulation. Finally, we look into three adaptions of RoPE - namely NTK aware RoPE, dynamic scaling and NTK by parts. Some of these innovations were in fact announced as Reddit posts! Lastly, we look at YaRN - \u201cYet Another rope extentioN\u201d method which combines NTK by parts with an introduction of temperature parameter to the attention formulation. Most modern LLMs today such as Qwen, DeepSeek, LLaMA, gpt-oss are finetuned using YaRN to enable context length expansion only utilising a small percentage of the pre-trained dataset.\n\nThe beauty of dynamic scaling is that it eliminates the need to choose between short and long sequence performance - the model automatically adapts to whatever it needs to process. In fact, this approach has been adopted in production: Qwen models use Dynamic NTK-aware scaling to achieve strong performance across context lengths (Qwen Team 2023). Qwen Team. 2023. \u201cQwen Technical Memo: Long Context Inference.\u201d https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen/blob/main/tech_memo.md#long-context-inference. After the initial NTK-aware scaling, bloc97 discovered that different frequency components benefit from different scaling strategies. This led to the \u201cby parts\u201d correction - a more sophisticated approach that applies different interpolation methods to different parts of the frequency spectrum. The key insight: RoPE\u2019s frequency components encode information at different scales: High frequencies (low dimensions): Encode local, fine-grained relationships \u00b7\n\nIn the world of agents, where context deeply affects, context lengths play a crucial role in supporting the industry reach new state of the art solutions and integrations with Large language models. The recent accomplishment from Tongyi Labs introducing Tongyi Deepresearch (DeepResearch Team, Tongyi Lab 2025) termed the 128K context length as insufficient! Often times using Claude Code, we run into context limits - where the conversation is then \u201ccompacted\u201d by the terminal agent. On the other hand, Gemini CLI has a massive 1M context length! While at longer context, the accuracy of retrieval reduces, there are techniques to suppress that and it is pretty handy to have larger context lengths. However, the improvement in context length didn\u2019t come from model architecture upgrades alone. In addition to the chosen attention mechanism, positional embeddings play a crucial role in long context modeling.",

        "text": "Contiguous RoPE enables transformers to extrapolate context to millions of tokens using innovative scaling laws, critical dimensions, and optimized positional techniques.\n\nToo small a base compresses periodicity, potentially reducing expressiveness and semantic discrimination; too large a base limits the reliably extrapolatable window due to under-trained high-frequency dimensions. Integrating log-scaled attention or dynamic scaling further impacts score stability and must be calibrated to avoid attention explosions. The emergence of \u201ccritical dimensions\u201d\u2014those for which a full period fits within the training window\u2014implies that position encoding in RoPE is fundamentally dimension-dependent. If the training window is short relative to the period of a given dimension, that dimension remains underutilized in downstream long-context inference (Men et al., 23 May 2024). Dimension-level analysis in (Chiang et al., 16 Feb 2025) supports this hypothesis: RoPE causes systematic underutilization of high-frequency dimensions in long-distance retrieval. Controlled experiments and inspection of LLMs (LLaMA, Qwen, OLMo) reveal that attention heads attach minimal impo\n\nThe scaling law framework established in (Liu et al., 2023) provides explicit equations relating rotary base, training length, and extrapolation bounds. For larger bases, the upper bound on contiguous extrapolation is given by: ... The approach leverages theoretical scaling laws and critical dimension analysis to balance base tuning and periodic encoding for stable long-context performance. Empirical studies on models like LLaMA2 demonstrate that evolutionary parameter search and optimized RoPE techniques yield robust performance on document-level and multi-document tasks. Contiguous RoPE describes the interplay of design choices, scaling laws, and empirical techniques for enabling transformer-based LLMs that employ Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) to process sequences far longer than the context observed during pre-training\u2014often spanning hundreds of thousands to millions of contiguous tokens.\n\nIt encompasses recent innovations in periodic parameter selection, extrapolation strategies, architectural modifications, and optimization principles, all rigorously grounded in mathematical analysis and large-scale experimental results. The concept fundamentally addresses long-context extrapolation, effective attention computation, and practical resource scaling in modern LLMs. Standard RoPE implements positional encoding by rotating query and key vectors in each 2-dimensional subspace using an angle ... nn the dimension index. This yields a distributed set of frequencies encoding both absolute and relative positions. Contiguous extension fails in vanilla RoPE because, in dimensions with long sinusoidal periods, training does not cover a full cycle; out-of-distribution (OOD) patterns arise during inference at positions much greater than the training context.",

      "id": "compass_artifact_wf-2dd2c9f6-ee59-4e21-9b4d-486cdb62480b_text/markdown",
      "type": "text/markdown",
      "title": "Local Semantic Keyword Extraction on Mac: A Practical Technology Guide",
      "source": "c",
      "command": "create",
      "content": "# Local semantic keyword extraction on Mac: a practical technology guide\n\nFor extracting maximum keywords\u2014including implicit and latent concepts\u2014from text documents on a Mac Studio with 64GB RAM and small LLMs like Qwen3-0.6B, **the optimal approach combines multiple complementary tools**: KeyBERT for embedding-based extraction, BERTopic for latent topic discovery, GLiNER for zero-shot entity recognition, and a small LLM for keyword expansion. All of these tools run efficiently on Apple Silicon using MLX or llama.cpp backends, with total memory requirements under 15GB\u2014well within your hardware capacity.\n\nThe key insight is that **no single tool excels at all aspects of keyword extraction**. Statistical methods (YAKE, RAKE) are fast but miss semantic nuances. Embedding-based tools (KeyBERT) capture semantic similarity but only extract explicit phrases. Topic models (BERTopic) discover latent themes but work at corpus level. LLMs can generate implicit keywords but are slower. A hybrid pipeline leveraging each tool's strengths delivers maximum keyword coverage while remaining efficient on local hardware.\n\n---\n\n## Keyword extraction libraries: from statistical to semantic\n\nThe landscape of keyword extraction divides into three categories: statistical methods requiring no ML infrastructure, embedding-based approaches using transformer models, and generative methods that can produce keyphrases not present in the source text.\n\n**YAKE** (Yet Another Keyword Extractor) stands as the fastest option, using statistical features like term frequency, position, and sentence distribution to score candidates. It processes documents in approximately **10ms per 1000 words** with under 50MB memory footprint. YAKE requires no external models or training, making it ideal for a first-pass extraction. However, it cannot capture semantic relationships\u2014\"machine learning\" and \"ML\" would be treated as completely unrelated.\n\n**KeyBERT** addresses this limitation by using sentence transformer embeddings. It embeds the entire document and candidate phrases, then selects candidates with highest cosine similarity to the document vector. The **Maximal Marginal Relevance (MMR)** option reduces redundancy by penalizing candidates similar to already-selected keywords. KeyBERT supports any sentence transformer model, including efficient options like **all-MiniLM-L6-v2** (23M parameters, 90MB) or ultralight **Model2Vec** variants under 10MB. A typical configuration achieves ~200ms per document with MMR enabled.\n\nFor generating keyphrases that don't appear in the text, **keyphrase generation models** offer a compelling alternative to pure extraction. The **ml6team/keyphrase-generation-t5-small** models (60M parameters, ~250MB) can produce absent keyphrases\u2014a document about \"deep neural networks\" might generate \"artificial intelligence\" even if those exact words don't appear. KeyBART from Bloomberg provides higher quality but larger size (~1.5GB).\n\n**PKE** (Python Keyphrase Extraction) bundles multiple algorithms under a unified API: graph-based methods like TextRank and TopicRank, plus statistical approaches. TopicRank clusters similar candidates before ranking, improving diversity. PKE integrates with spaCy for POS-based candidate selection, focusing on noun phrases rather than arbitrary n-grams.\n\n---\n\n## BERTopic leads topic modeling for latent concept discovery\n\nTopic modeling excels at discovering latent semantic structures across document collections, surfacing concepts that characterize texts even when those concepts aren't explicitly stated. **BERTopic** has emerged as the dominant choice, combining modern embedding models with interpretable topic representations.\n\nBERTopic's architecture flows through five stages: embedding documents via sentence transformers, reducing dimensionality with **UMAP** (typically 384 \u2192 5 dimensions), clustering with **HDBSCAN**, then extracting topic keywords using **class-based TF-IDF (c-TF-IDF)**. The c-TF-IDF algorithm treats all documents in a cluster as one merged document, identifying words that are frequent within a topic but rare across the corpus.\n\nThe **KeyBERTInspired representation model** significantly improves keyword quality. Rather than relying solely on c-TF-IDF scores, it extracts representative documents per topic, generates topic embeddings by averaging these documents, then selects keywords with highest semantic similarity to the topic centroid. This produces more coherent, less stopword-contaminated keywords.\n\nFor local deployment, BERTopic supports **Model2Vec embeddings** for CPU-only operation without GPU dependencies. Memory requirements scale with corpus size\u2014plan for **4-8GB for 10,000-100,000 documents**. Document-level keywords can be extracted via `get_document_info()`, which returns topic assignments and representative words per document.\n\n**Top2Vec** offers an alternative approach where document, word, and topic vectors share a joint embedding space. Topic keywords are simply the n-closest word vectors to each topic centroid\u2014a more intuitive semantic relationship than c-TF-IDF. Top2Vec automatically determines topic count, useful for exploration. Recent work adds **Contextual Top2Vec** for token-level topic assignments.\n\nClassical **LDA** (Latent Dirichlet Allocation) via Gensim remains valuable for interpretability and efficiency. It processes documents in streaming fashion with constant memory, runs entirely on CPU, and produces probabilistic topic-word distributions that many downstream applications expect. For large corpora where neural approaches become memory-constrained, LDA scales gracefully.\n\n---\n\n## GLiNER enables zero-shot entity extraction with custom labels\n\nNamed entity recognition contributes explicit entities\u2014people, organizations, products, technologies\u2014that form natural keywords. Traditional NER tools like **spaCy** provide production-ready extraction with pre-trained models: `en_core_web_sm` (12MB) handles common entity types at ~30MB runtime memory, while `en_core_web_trf` (RoBERTa-based) offers higher accuracy at ~2-4GB.\n\nThe breakthrough tool is **GLiNER** (Generalist Lightweight NER), published at NAACL 2024. GLiNER extracts **arbitrary entity types specified at runtime** without retraining. You provide labels like [\"technology\", \"algorithm\", \"framework\", \"concept\"] and GLiNER identifies matching spans. This enables domain-specific keyword extraction without building custom training datasets.\n\nGLiNER uses a bidirectional DeBERTa encoder with parallel entity extraction, achieving **faster inference than sequential LLM generation** while matching or exceeding GPT-4's zero-shot NER performance. The **NuNER_Zero** variant outperforms GLiNER Large by 3% on benchmarks. Models range from ~300MB (small) to ~1GB (large), all Apache 2.0 licensed.\n\n```python\nfrom gliner import GLiNER\nmodel = GLiNER.from_pretrained(\"urchade/gliner_medium-v2.1\")\nlabels = [\"technology\", \"methodology\", \"tool\", \"concept\", \"framework\"]\nentities = model.predict_entities(text, labels, threshold=0.5)\n```\n\nFor discovering implicit relationships between entities, **REBEL** (Relation Extraction By End-to-end Language generation) extracts (subject, relation, object) triplets. Built on BART, it recognizes over 200 Wikidata relation types. These relations reveal implicit keywords: a document mentioning \"Yann LeCun\" might yield triplets indicating \"works at Meta AI\" and \"pioneer of convolutional networks\"\u2014generating \"Meta AI\" and \"convolutional networks\" as implicit keywords even if unstated.\n\n---\n\n## Zero-shot classification tags documents with topic labels\n\nZero-shot classification assigns topic labels to documents without task-specific training, enabling automated topic tagging that surfaces implicit themes. The standard approach uses natural language inference (NLI) models: frame classification as entailment between the document and hypothesis \"This text is about {topic}.\"\n\n**DistilBART-MNLI** models from the valhalla series offer the best size/accuracy tradeoff. The **distilbart-mnli-12-3** variant achieves 88% accuracy at ~1GB\u2014compared to bart-large-mnli's 90% at 1.6GB. However, NLI-based classification scales as O(N\u00d7K) with N documents and K labels.\n\nFor efficiency with many labels, **sentence transformer zero-shot** scales as **O(N+K)**. Embed documents and topic labels separately, then classify by cosine similarity. The all-MiniLM-L6-v2 model (127MB) processes thousands of labels efficiently. This approach enables hierarchical topic discovery: start with broad categories, identify high-confidence matches, then narrow with subcategory labels.\n\n```python\nfrom sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer\nmodel = SentenceTransformer('all-MiniLM-L6-v2')\ndoc_emb = model.encode(document)\ntopic_embs = model.encode([\"technology\", \"finance\", \"healthcare\"])\nsimilarities = cosine_similarity([doc_emb], topic_embs)\n```\n\n---\n\n## Apple Silicon optimization: MLX and llama.cpp deliver native performance\n\nMac Studio's unified memory architecture provides unique advantages for ML workloads: **CPU and GPU share the same memory pool**, eliminating data transfer overhead that bottlenecks discrete GPU systems. With 64GB unified memory, approximately **48GB is accessible to the GPU** by default.\n\n**MLX** is Apple's native ML framework, achieving highest sustained throughput on Apple Silicon. Benchmarks show Qwen-2.5 models running at **230+ tokens/second** with 5-7ms median per-token latency on M2 Ultra. MLX-LM handles LLM inference with native quantization support, while **mlx-embeddings** provides sentence transformer inference\u2014both with zero-copy memory operations.\n\nFor broader model compatibility, **llama.cpp** with Metal backend delivers excellent performance. Token generation is **memory bandwidth bound**\u2014the Mac Studio M2 Max achieves ~66 tokens/second on LLaMA 7B Q4_0, while M3/M4 Max variants reach 83+ t/s due to higher bandwidth. Prompt processing scales with GPU core count. The **Q4_K_M quantization** offers the best quality/speed tradeoff.\n\n**Ollama** wraps llama.cpp with convenient model management: `ollama pull qwen3:0.6b` downloads and configures models automatically. Enable Metal acceleration via `OLLAMA_METAL_ENABLED=1`. Typical 8B models achieve 28-35 tokens/second on M3 Pro/Max.\n\nFor embedding models, several deployment options exist:\n- **MLX-native**: mlx-embeddings or mlx_embedding_models for maximum Apple Silicon performance\n- **Sentence Transformers with MPS**: PyTorch's Metal Performance Shaders backend, set `PYTORCH_ENABLE_MPS_FALLBACK=1` for unsupported operations\n- **ONNX Runtime with CoreML**: `MLComputeUnits: ALL` distributes work across CPU, GPU, and Neural Engine\n\n---\n\n## Recommended model configurations for 64GB Mac Studio\n\nGiven 64GB unified memory, you can comfortably run multiple models simultaneously. Here are tested configurations:\n\n| Configuration | Embedding Model | LLM | Total Memory | Use Case |\n|--------------|-----------------|-----|--------------|----------|\n| Lightweight | all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (90MB) | Qwen3-0.6B Q4 (400MB) | ~3GB | Fast processing, high volume |\n| Balanced | BGE-small-en (130MB) | Qwen3-1.7B Q4 (1GB) | ~5GB | Good quality, reasonable speed |\n| High Quality | BGE-base-en (440MB) | Qwen3-4B Q4 (2.5GB) | ~8GB | Best small-model quality |\n| Maximum | BGE-M3 (2GB) | Qwen3-8B Q4 (5GB) | ~12GB | Near-commercial quality |\n\nThe **balanced configuration leaves 55+ GB** for document processing, BERTopic training, and system overhead\u2014more than sufficient for production workloads.\n\n---\n\n## Integration patterns: combining tools for maximum coverage\n\nThe most effective approach **layers multiple extraction methods**, each contributing different keyword types:\n\n**Pattern 1: Parallel extraction pipeline**\nRun KeyBERT, YAKE, and NER simultaneously on each document. KeyBERT captures semantic keywords, YAKE identifies statistical patterns (potentially different from KeyBERT), and GLiNER extracts domain-specific entities. Merge results with optional deduplication by embedding similarity.\n\n**Pattern 2: Embedding clustering with LLM expansion**\nFor large document collections, cluster documents by embedding similarity (threshold ~0.75 cosine similarity). Extract keywords only from cluster representatives using the small LLM, then assign those keywords to all cluster members. This reduces LLM calls by 80-90% while maintaining coverage.\n\n**Pattern 3: Topic-enriched document keywords**\nTrain BERTopic on your corpus to discover latent topics. For each document, combine KeyBERT-extracted keywords with the topic keywords from that document's assigned topic. This adds latent/implicit concepts that KeyBERT wouldn't discover from the single document.\n\n```python\n# Hybrid pipeline pseudocode\nexplicit_keywords = keybert.extract_keywords(doc, use_mmr=True)\nentities = gliner.predict_entities(doc, custom_labels)\ntopic_keywords = bertopic.get_topic(bertopic.transform([doc])[0])\n\n# LLM expansion for implicit concepts\nexpansion_prompt = f\"Given keywords {explicit_keywords}, suggest 5 related concepts:\"\nimplicit_keywords = llm.generate(expansion_prompt)\n\nall_keywords = merge_and_deduplicate(explicit_keywords, entities, \n                                      topic_keywords, implicit_keywords)\n```\n\n**Pattern 4: KeyLLM hybrid**\nKeyBERT's **KeyLLM** integration passes extracted keywords to an LLM for refinement. Configure with local Ollama backend: the LLM can merge similar keywords, add missing concepts, and filter irrelevant extractions. This produces more coherent final keyword sets than pure extraction.\n\n---\n\n## Practical implementation recommendations\n\nFor your Mac Studio deployment targeting deep semantic keyword extraction:\n\n**Start with this stack:**\n- **YAKE** for fast statistical baseline (~10ms/document)\n- **KeyBERT + all-MiniLM-L6-v2** for semantic extraction (~200ms/document)\n- **GLiNER medium** for zero-shot entity extraction with custom types (~100ms/document)\n- **BERTopic** trained on your corpus for latent topic keywords (one-time training)\n- **Qwen3-0.6B via Ollama/llama.cpp** for keyword expansion and implicit concept generation\n\n**For maximum latent concept discovery**, BERTopic with KeyBERTInspired representation model is essential. The combination of UMAP dimensionality reduction and HDBSCAN clustering surfaces semantic structures invisible to single-document methods. Set `calculate_probabilities=True` to get soft topic assignments revealing documents that bridge multiple themes.\n\n**For keyphrase generation** (truly absent keywords), add the T5-small keyphrase generation model. At 60M parameters and ~250MB, it's lightweight enough to run alongside other models and generates keyphrases the document \"should\" have based on its content.\n\n**Memory management**: Load models lazily and unload when not in use. With 64GB, you won't hit limits with the recommended stack, but careful management enables running even larger models if needed. Use `gc.collect()` after unloading models to release memory promptly.\n\nAll recommended tools are production-ready, actively maintained, and MIT or Apache 2.0 licensed (except REBEL, which is CC BY-NC-SA). KeyBERT and BERTopic share the same author (Maarten Grootendorst) and integrate seamlessly. The ecosystem is mature: expect straightforward pip installations without dependency conflicts on modern Python (3.10+).\n\n---\n\n## Conclusion\n\nBuilding a comprehensive keyword extraction system for Mac Studio requires combining complementary approaches rather than seeking a single solution. **KeyBERT + BERTopic + GLiNER + small LLM** covers the full spectrum from explicit extraction to latent discovery to generative expansion. \n\nThe 64GB unified memory architecture is ideal for this workload\u2014run all models simultaneously with headroom to spare. MLX and llama.cpp deliver native Apple Silicon performance without the complexity of CUDA environments. The recommended balanced configuration (BGE-small + Qwen3-1.7B) provides excellent quality at under 5GB memory, leaving substantial capacity for scaling up.\n\nThe critical insight for latent keyword discovery: **topic modeling on your corpus is non-negotiable**. Single-document methods, no matter how sophisticated, cannot discover themes that emerge only from seeing many documents. BERTopic with KeyBERTInspired provides this capability with modern embedding quality and interpretable results. Combined with LLM-powered expansion, you'll extract keywords that characterize documents even when those words never appear in the text.",

HybridKeywordExtractor:\n    def __init__(self):\n        # \u521d\u59cb\u5316\u6240\u6709\u6a21\u578b\n        self.yake = yake.KeywordExtractor(lan=\"zh\", n=3, top=30)\n        self.keybert = \"\u5de5\u5177\", \"\u4ea7\u54c1\"]\n        \n    def extract(self, text, use_llm=True):\n        results = {\n            \"statistical\": [],\n            \"semantic\": [],\n            \"entities\": [],\n            \"implicit\": []\n        }\n        \n        # 1. \u7edf\u8ba1\u5173\u952e\u8bcd (YAKE)\n        yake_kws = self.yake.extract_keywords(text)\n        results[\"statistical\"] = [kw for kw, score in yake_kws]\n        \n        # 2. \u8bed\u4e49\u5173\u952e\u8bcd (KeyBERT)\n        bert_kws = self.keybert.extract_keywords(\n            text, use_mmr=True, diversity=0.7, top_n=20\n        )\n        results[\"semantic\"] = [kw for kw, score in bert_kws]\n        \n        # 3. \u5b9e\u4f53 (GLiNER)\n        entities = self.gliner.predict_entities(\n            text, self.entity_labels, threshold=0.5\n        )\n        results[\"entities\"] = [e[\"text\"] for e in entities]\n        \n        # 4. \u9690\u542b\u5173\u952e\u8bcd (LLM) - \u53ef\u9009\n        if use_llm:\n            explicit = results[\"statistical\"] + results[\"semantic\"]\n            results[\"implicit\"] = self._llm_expand(explicit, text)\n        \n        # 5. \u53bb\u91cd\u5408\u5e76\n        all_keywords = self._merge_and_dedupe(results)\n        return all_keywords\n    \n    def _llm_expand(self, keywords, context):\n        # \u8c03\u7528\u672c\u5730 LLM\n        pass\n    \n    def _merge_and_dedupe(self, results):\n        # \u57fa\u4e8e\u5d4c\u5165\u76f8\u4f3c\u5ea6\u53bb\u91cd\n        pass\n```\n\n---\n\n## \u5185\u5b58\u914d\u7f6e\u5efa\u8bae\n\n### 64GB Mac Studio \u63a8\u8350\u914d\u7f6e\n\n| \u914d\u7f6e\u7ea7\u522b | \u5d4c\u5165\u6a21\u578b | LLM | \u603b\u5185\u5b58 |        # \\u8fd4\\u56de\\u524d20\\u4e2a\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\n)\\nkeywords = kw_extractor.extract_keywords(text)\\n```\\n\\n**\\u7279\\u70b9**\\uff1a\\n- \\u901f\\u5ea6\\uff1a~10ms / 1000\\u8bcd\\n- \\u7b2c\\u4e8c\\u5c42\\uff1a\\u8bed\\u4e49\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\u63d0\\u53d6\\n\\n### KeyBERT\\uff08\\u63a8\\u8350\\uff09\\n\\n```bash\\npip install keybert |\\n| BGE-small-zh | 130MB | \\u2b50\\u2b50\\u2b50\\u2b50 | \\u4e2d\\u6587\\u4f18\\u5316 |\\n| BGE-M3 | 2GB | \\u2b50\\u2b50\\u2b50\\u2b50\\u2b50 | \\u6700\\u9ad8\\u8d28\\u91cf |\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \\u7b2c\\u4e09\\u5c42\\uff1a\\u6f5c\\u5728\\u4e3b\\u9898\\u53d1\\u73b0\\n\\n### BERTopic\\uff08\\u53d1\\u73b0\\u9690\\u542b\\u6982\\u5ff5\\u7684\\u5173\\u952e\\uff09\\n\\n```bash\\npip install bertopic\\n```\\n\\n```python\\nfrom bertopic import BERTopic\\nfrom bertopic.representation import KeyBERTInspired\\n\\n# \\u4f7f\\u7528 KeyBERTInspired \\u63d0\\u5347\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\u8d28\\u91cf\\nrepresentation_model = KeyBERTInspired()\\n\\ntopic_model = BERTopic(\\n    embedding_model=\\\"all-MiniLM-L6-v2\\\",\\n    representation_model=representation_model,\\n    calculate_probabilities=True,  # \\u8f6f\\u4e3b\\u9898\\u5206\\u914d\\n    verbose=True\\n)\\n\\n# \\u8bad\\u7ec3\\uff08\\u9700\\u8981\\u6587\\u6863\\u96c6\\u5408\\uff09\\ntopics, probs = topic_model.fit_transform(documents)\\n\\n# \\u83b7\\u53d6\\u6bcf\\u4e2a\\u6587\\u6863\\u7684\\u4e3b\\u9898\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\ndoc_info = topic_model.get_document_info(documents)\\n```\\n\\n**BERTopic \\u5de5\\u4f5c\\u6d41\\u7a0b**\\uff1a\\n```\\n\\u6587\\u6863 \\u2192 \\u5d4c\\u5165(Sentence Transformer) \\u2192 \\u964d\\u7ef4(UMAP) \\u2192 \\u805a\\u7c7b(HDBSCAN) \\u2192 c-TF-IDF \\u2192 \\u4e3b\\u9898\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\n```\\n\\n**\\u4e3a\\u4ec0\\u4e48 BERTopic \\u80fd\\u53d1\\u73b0\\u9690\\u542b\\u6982\\u5ff5**\\uff1a\\n- \\u5355\\u6587\\u6863\\u65b9\\u6cd5\\u53ea\\u80fd\\u770b\\u5230\\u4e00\\u7bc7\\u6587\\u6863\\n- \\u4e3b\\u9898\\u6a21\\u578b\\u770b\\u5230\\u6574\\u4e2a\\u8bed\\u6599\\u5e93\\u7684\\u6a21\\u5f0f\\n- \\u80fd\\u53d1\\u73b0\\u8de8\\u6587\\u6863\\u7684\\u8bed\\u4e49\\u7ed3\\u6784\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \\u7b2c\\u56db\\u5c42\\uff1a\\u96f6\\u6837\\u672c\\u5b9e\\u4f53\\u63d0\\u53d6\\n\\n### GLiNER\\uff08\\u81ea\\u5b9a\\u4e49\\u5b9e\\u4f53\\u7c7b\\u578b\\uff09\\n\\n```bash\\npip install gliner\\n```\\n\\n```python\\nfrom gliner import GLiNER\\n\\nmodel = GLiNER.from_pretrained(\\\"urchade/gliner_medium-v2.1\\\")\\n\\n# \\u81ea\\u5b9a\\u4e49\\u4f60\\u60f3\\u63d0\\u53d6\\u7684\\u5b9e\\u4f53\\u7c7b\\u578b\\nlabels = [\\n    \\\"\\u6280\\u672f\\\", \\\"\\u7b97\\u6cd5\\\", \\\"\\u6846\\u67b6\\\", \\\"\\u6982\\u5ff5\\\", \\n    \\\"\\u65b9\\u6cd5\\u8bba\\\", \\\"\\u5de5\\u5177\\\", \\\"\\u4ea7\\u54c1\\\", \\\"\\u516c\\u53f8\\\"\\n]\\n\\nentities = model.predict_entities(\\n    text, \\n    labels, \\n    threshold=0.5\\n)\\n\\n# \\u8f93\\u51fa: [{\\\"text\\\": \\\"\\u6df1\\u5ea6\\u5b66\\u4e60\\\", \\\"label\\\": \\\"\\u6280\\u672f\\\", \\\"score\\\": 0.92}, ...]\\n```\\n\\n**GLiNER \\u4f18\\u52bf**\\uff1a\\n- \\u96f6\\u6837\\u672c\\uff1a\\u65e0\\u9700\\u8bad\\u7ec3\\uff0c\\u8fd0\\u884c\\u65f6\\u6307\\u5b9a\\u5b9e\\u4f53\\u7c7b\\u578b\\n- \\u901f\\u5ea6\\uff1a~100ms/\\u6587\\u6863\\uff0c\\u6bd4 LLM \\u5feb\\u5f97\\u591a\\n- \\u8d28\\u91cf\\uff1a\\u5339\\u914d GPT-4 \\u7684\\u96f6\\u6837\\u672c NER \\u6027\\u80fd\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \\u7b2c\\u4e94\\u5c42\\uff1aLLM \\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\u6269\\u5c55\\n\\n### \\u4f7f\\u7528 Qwen3-0.6B \\u751f\\u6210\\u9690\\u542b\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\n\\n```python\\nimport requests\\n\\ndef expand_keywords(explicit_keywords, context):\\n    prompt = f\\\"\\\"\\\"\\u57fa\\u4e8e\\u4ee5\\u4e0b\\u4ece\\u6587\\u6863\\u4e2d\\u63d0\\u53d6\\u7684\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\uff1a\\n{explicit_keywords}\\n\\n\\u4ee5\\u53ca\\u6587\\u6863\\u4e0a\\u4e0b\\u6587\\uff1a\\n{context[:500]}\\n\\n\\u8bf7\\u751f\\u6210 10 \\u4e2a\\u76f8\\u5173\\u4f46\\u672a\\u5728\\u539f\\u6587\\u4e2d\\u51fa\\u73b0\\u7684\\u6982\\u5ff5\\u3001\\u4e3b\\u9898\\u6216\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd\\uff1a\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\n    response = requests.post(\\n        \\\"http://localhost:8080/completion\\\",\\n        json={\\n            \\\"prompt\\\": prompt,\\n            \\\"n_predict\\\": 200,\\n            \\\"temperature\\\": 0.7\\n        }\\n    )\\n    return response.json()[\\\"content\\\"]\\n```\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \\u5b8c\\u6574\\u6df7\\u5408\\u6d41\\u6c34\\u7ebf\\n\\n```python\\nfrom keybert import KeyBERT\\nfrom gliner import GLiNER\\nimport yake\\n\\nclass HybridKeywordExtractor:\\n    def __init__(self):\\n        # \\u521d\\u59cb\\u5316\\u6240\\u6709\\u6a21\\u578b\\n        self.yake = yake.KeywordExtractor(lan=\\\"zh\\\", n=3, top=30)\\n        self.keybert = KeyBERT(model='all-MiniLM-L6-v2')\\n        self.gliner = GLiNER.from_pretrained(\\\"urchade/gliner_medium-v2.1\\\")\\n        self.entity_labels = [\\\"\\u6280\\u672f\\\", \\\"\\u6982\\u5ff5\\\", \\\"\\u65b9\\u6cd5\\\", \\\"\\u5de5\\u5177\\\", \\\"\\u4ea7\\u54c1\\\"]\\n        \\n    def extract(self, text, use_llm=True):\\n        results = {\\n            \\\"statistical\\\": [],\\n            \\\"semantic\\\": [],\\n            \\\"entities\\\": [],\\n            \\\"implicit\\\": []\\n        }\\n        \\n        # 1. \\u7edf\\u8ba1\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd (YAKE)\\n        yake_kws = self.yake.extract_keywords(text)\\n        results[\\\"statistical\\\"] = [kw for kw, score in yake_kws]\\n        \\n        # 2. \\u8bed\\u4e49\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd (KeyBERT)\\n        bert_kws = self.keybert.extract_keywords(\\n            text, use_mmr=True, diversity=0.7, top_n=20\\n        )\\n        results[\\\"semantic\\\"] = [kw for kw, score in bert_kws]\\n        \\n        # 3. \\u5b9e\\u4f53 (GLiNER)\\n        entities = self.gliner.predict_entities(\\n            text, self.entity_labels, threshold=0.5\\n        )\\n        results[\\\"entities\\\"] = [e[\\\"text\\\"] for e in entities]\\n        \\n        # 4. \\u9690\\u542b\\u5173\\u952e\\u8bcd (LLM) - \\u53ef\\u9009\\n        if use_llm:\\n            explicit = results[\\\"statistical\\\"] + results[\\\"semantic\\\"]\\n            results[\\\"implicit\\\"] = self._llm_expand(explicit, text)\\n        \\n        # 5. \\u53bb\\u91cd\\u5408\\u5e76\\n        all_keywords = self._merge_and_dedupe(results)\\n        return all_keywords\\n    \\n    def _llm_expand(self, keywords, context):\\n        # \\u8c03\\u7528\\u672c\\u5730 LLM\\n        pass\\n    \\n    def _merge_and_dedupe(self, results):\\n        # \\u57fa\\u4e8e\\u5d4c\\u5165\\u76f8\\u4f3c\\u5ea6\\u53bb\\u91cd\\n        pass\\n```\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \\u5185\\u5b58\\u914d\\u7f6e\\u5efa\\u8bae\\n\\n### 64GB Mac Studio \\u63a8\\u8350\\u914d\\u7f6e\\n\\n| \\u914d\\u7f6e\\u7ea7\\u522b | \\u5d4c\\u5165\\u6a21\\u578b | LLM |
